Ratios between devices are measured and correct whatever the reference. | Run the bench on the reference machine (AC-ProArt, `/harness/` › Run the bench) and set the three figures from its drawing row; then re-state each pass's declared cost on that machine from the performance readout. | to measure |
| `probe.ts` `ULTRA_HEADROOM` (VP4) | Until a recipe declares an Ultra form, a device earns Ultra when its High standard frame is predicted within 30% of the budget (twice the room a High placement needs). | No Ultra pass exists yet to predict; the device's class still has to be known for the badge and the bench. | The Ultra recipe's own predicted frame (placement already uses it as soon as one is declared). | tuned rule, replaced by the first Ultra recipe |
| `renderer/src/runtime.ts` `passShares` (VP4) | The performance readout's per-pass lines split the globe's measured drawing time by each pass's declared cost (scaled by the probe), labelled "estimated share". | The GPU is timed per frame, not per pass, on every device. | Timestamp queries around each pass where the device has them (WebGPU `timestamp-query`), falling back to the estimate elsewhere. | labelled in the readout |
| `renderer/src/finish.ts` read limit (VP4) | Every read of the scene in the shared finish is held under 60,000 (half-float tops out at 65,504) before the glare and the enlarger. | A star's point or the sun's disc can pass half-float's top; an infinity through the enlarger's negative lobes or a zero weight is NaN, which the glare spreads over the picture as black. Anything that bright is white after tone mapping anyway. | A full-float scene target where the device has it, or light pre-scaled by the exposure before it is stored. | guard |
| O1 test `light.test.ts:20-26` | Expected colours from Mitchell Charity's table, within ±10/255. | A published reference for blackbody sRGB. | Keep, but verify the table values (unverified this session), or compute them from the CIE 1931 tabulated functions rather than the Wyman fit. | values unverified |
| `renderer/src/ladder.ts` detail ladder (VP6), every drawn rung | Detail finer than the kernel resolves is noise (simplex, curl, cells, ridges) whose largest size, amplitude and speed are kernel values; its pattern is invented. Grade "drawn from the numbers" (visual polish design 3): never clickable, named or read by a rule; the HUD's detail line says where facts end. | No block resolves eddies, stones or ice cracks yet, and a browser could not run one per pixel. | Each layer's own block when it exists (eddy-resolving weather, a regolith and crater-population model, a fracture model), drawn as facts. | stand-in per layer, permanent as a mechanism |
| `ladder.ts` `EDDY_EXPONENT` | A smaller rung's eddies move at speed × (size / largest)^(1/3) (Kolmogorov 1941 inertial range), so its gradients turn faster. | One cheap rule for every flowing layer. Giant planets' eddies are not 3D Kolmogorov turbulence (2D inverse cascade, Rhines arrest). | The layer's measured or modelled velocity spectrum, from the weather block. | stand-in |
| `ladder.ts` `FLOW_REACH`, two-phase flow (Vlachos 2010) | Advected detail is carried half a cell, then cross-faded with a copy half a cycle behind. | Carried detail would otherwise smear without limit. | Detail advected by the real wind field over the time drawn. | permanent (drawing technique) |
| `ladder.ts` `MAX_STRETCH` = 64 | Stretch (features longer across an axis than along it) is capped at 64. | Stretch multiplies shader coordinates; past about 64 float32 precision suffers and the features are threads anyway. | None needed: a ring's fine structure past that is drawn by its profile. | permanent with reason |
| `renderer/src/ladder-layers.json` starters | Shape numbers: Hurst exponents (giant curls 1/3, clouds 0.5, ground 0.8, craters 0, ice 0.5, rings 0.5); giant curls stretched by Rhines scale over deformation radius and moved by shear over jet speed; ring structure stretched by radius over profile spacing. | Starters so the owner threads have a working declaration; each owner tunes its own. | Fits to measured spectra (Cassini and Juno images for giant eddies, lunar and martian roughness spectra for ground, ring occultation power spectra). | stand-in until each owner's package |
| `renderer/src/gpu.ts` texture ladder tier (VP3) | The face size's cap is set by the graphics interface (WebGPU: high, to 2048; WebGL2: basic, to 512), not by measured speed. | The speed probe (`probe.ts`, VP4) is not built yet. | The probe's placement (visual polish design 8.2) passes the measured tier to `draw`. | stand-in until VP4 |
| `renderer/src/gpu.ts` bake size (VP3) | A bake is no finer than four texels per tile sample, and is kept while within a factor of two of the wanted size. | Finer adds nothing the GPU's filtering does not; re-baking on every small change of size would stutter. | None needed (a drawing rule). | permanent with reason |
| `renderer/src/texture-ladder-gpu.ts` deep patch edge (VP3 part 4) | The deep patch is eased into the globe's own faces over its outer seventh, so the finer ground has no hard edge. | Only the tiles round the point under the camera are read finely; a hard edge would draw a square the world does not have. | None needed (a camera effect: both sides are the kernel's ground). | permanent with reason |
| `renderer/src/ground-rows.json` (VP3 part 5) | Names which data sheet reflectance rows each kernel ground material reads in True (regolith and sediment: mature highland soil 62231; basalt: fresh 12030 to mature 10084 mare soil). | The rows belong on the materials in packs/base-surface, but editing that pack changes its content hash and so the golden; it moves in the golden lane. Reading 62231 for 'sediment' is this thread's choice, not the data's: where space reaches the ground the kernel's sediment is impact-gardened fines, the same soil as regolith. The LSCC fine fractions (10084, 12030) run brighter than Pieters' 62231 standard, so the two sets are never blended as one material's fresh and mature. | `packs/base-surface` materials name their reflectance rows; the kernel picks a soil by the crust's chemistry. | stand-in until base-surface names its rows (golden lane) |
| `renderer/src/ground-optics.ts` highland soil (VP3 part 5) | Highland ground is drawn as mature soil 62231 at every maturity: fresh highland soil is not on the sheet yet, and its spectrum is not guessed from the mature one. | Only one highland spectrum has been sourced (Pieters 1999); the immature Apollo 16 soils have no published maturity found yet. | A fresh highland row (the data sheet's next MP35 part), then the same log blend as the mare soils. | stand-in until an immature highland row |
| `renderer/src/ground-optics.ts` ground with no weathering age (VP3 part 5, MATCH1) | A bare-ground sample whose tile carries no `maturity` (every world until SW1, and worlds with air) is drawn with its material's mature row. Ice (`h2o` n + ik) is drawn as a thick layer of 50 µm grains (sheet-optics.ts GRAIN_UM). | The kernel gives no weathering age or grain size yet; old exposed ground is mostly mature, and one shared base row beats a typed palette per material. On a world with air the coat is its stains (TRUE4), not space weathering. | SW1's `maturity` on every bare sample, a per-world weathering process for worlds with air, and grain sizes from the kernel. | stand-in until SW1 and grain sizes |
| `ui-contract/src/globe.ts` natural outlines (Alex 25 Nov, "a bit bitmap") | Between the kernel's cells, coasts and ice edges are drawn as smooth contours of the four nearest samples' blended shares, moved by up to 0.3 by fractal value noise keyed only on the direction (`sampleNatural`, `edgeNoise`, `naturalEdge`, `naturalDirection`), and bare ground's colour is averaged over bare neighbours two cells each way (`softenGround`). A sample's centre always shows its own fact; covers and kernel values are never changed. | The kernel's surface is cells; from a distance real coasts and soils are not square. The noise is invented detail, not data. | Sub-cell coasts from the kernel itself (a coastline field or finer tiles where they are read); an ice edge that follows height and temperature, not the climate's latitude band. | presentation stand-in |
| `renderer/src/ground-optics.ts` reflectance as colour (VP3 part 5) | The lab reflectance factor (light in at 30°, seen from straight above) is used as the ground's linear albedo at 650, 550 and 450 nm, lit as a Lambertian surface. | True lights ground with one physical BRDF for all materials; regolith's opposition surge and its fall-off with phase (Hapke) are not drawn. | A Hapke photometric function per material (single-scattering albedo and the opposition terms) in the light track's shading. | to review with the light thread |
| `renderer/src/texture-ladder.ts` `composeFaces` | Coarser tiles fill parts of a face not read finer, repeated up (nearest), and seas are flat at height 0 for the relief. | Tiles arrive a few at a time; a hole would be worse than a coarse patch. The sea surface is level; its floor's relief is not seen from space. | None for the fill; a sea's own surface (waves, tides) is the seas track's. | permanent with reason |

### 2. Candidates to move into flows (physics chain blocks)

The owner wants the physics in chain blocks (except big blocks) with a UI element that shows it. Each row below is something the TS presentation layer computes today that is physics and could be a block.

| Where | What it computes | Blocks it would need | View that would show it |
|---|---|---|---|
| `light.ts` `describeLight` (stars) | A star's spectrum (now a blackbody at Teff, weight 1). | `star.teff_k`, `star.radius_sun` (exist) → **`light.stellar-spectrum`** (Planck now; grid lookup later, results-as-data) → `light.description` fact (continuum components and lines). | A **spectrum chart** in the body card: log-λ axis, continuum curves, band strips, line ticks, the recipe's channels drawn over it, plus the "Why?" chain. The Eye and band recipes on the system chart read the same fact. |
| `light.ts` `describeLight` (planets) | Reflected starlight plus own heat. | `planet.bond_albedo`, `orbit.a`, star spectrum, `surface_k` → **`light.reflected`** (dilution (R*/a)², phase function, geometric albedo) + **`light.thermal-emission`** (emissivity, brightness temperature) → `light.description`. | The same spectrum chart, with the reflected and thermal parts as two stacked curves and the crossover wavelength marked. This shows directly why planets "come closer to their star" in mid-IR. |
| `light.ts` `xyz`, `linearSrgb`, `thermalColour` | Colour of a spectrum to the eye. | Colour is presentation, so keep it in TS. But a **`light.apparent-colour`** block (CIE XYZ, chromaticity x, y) is worth having as a fact so that Minerva's text tier and the players' facts can say "orange-white" from the same number. | A chromaticity swatch with a "CIE x, y" readout in the body card. A small CIE diagram with the Planckian locus would show where each star sits. |
| `light.ts` `teffFromSpectral` | Teff from a spectral-type string for players' scenes. | **`star.teff-from-spectral-type`** (pack table by class and luminosity class), run on disclosed facts only. | The players' chart colours and the players' body card ("Teff ≈ 5100 K, from type K1 V, estimated"). |
| `light.ts` Draper cut | Whether a thermal source is visibly glowing. | **`light.visible-glow`**: visible-band luminance at the surface (and later at an observer) against the photopic threshold. | An Eye recipe legend note ("too cool to glow"), and lava worlds or brown dwarfs on the chart glowing for a reason. |
| `compare.ts` `starColour` | Star disc colour on the Sizes tab. | Reuse the `light.apparent-colour` fact, or `thermalColour(teff)` directly. | The Sizes tab discs, matching the Eye recipe exactly. |
| `sound.ts` `orbitScene` period | Orbital period from a and M when no year fact exists. | Already a kernel quantity (orbit flows). Read `orbit.period_s` and stop recomputing in TS. | The sound "Why?" panel names the period fact and its chain. |
| V14 measure | Distance between two bodies now, and min/max for co-orbiting bodies. | An **`orbit.position-at-epoch`** (ephemeris) block, then a separation (light time = d/c). | The Measure panel on the chart, with a "Why?" naming the ephemeris block and its assumptions. |
| `reference-objects.json` brown dwarf radius | A typical radius. | **`substellar.radius-from-mass-age`** (evolution grid, results-as-data), already needed by system formation. | The Sizes tab: "typical brown dwarf, 40 M_J at 5 Gyr" with the radius computed, not typed. |
| light.recipes.json `why` texts (Wien, Rayleigh–Jeans) | Prose physics claims. | None (prose). The spectrum chart could draw λ_peak = b/T and mark the R–J tail, so the claim is visible. | Spectrum chart annotations. |

**UI element to visualise the chain.** One **Spectrum** panel per body (and per star) does most of this. It is a log-wavelength plot of the light description from its blocks, with the fixed band set as shaded strips, emission lines as ticks, and the active band recipe's channels overlaid. Under it are the block chain (stellar spectrum → reflected + thermal → description) and each block's "Why?". It shows what every recipe is integrating, makes stubbed parts visible (dashed curves labelled "stubbed"), and is the natural home for O2 and O4 evidence (brightness temperatures, line ratios).
| `gpu-air.ts`, `canvas.ts` | No aerosols (Mie haze) and no absorption (ozone, methane) | Not built yet | Mie scattering from the weather op's aerosols; absorption bands from the light track | Stand-in until R2b |
| `gpu-air.ts` | Single scattering only | Exact for thin air, no lookup tables to build | Hillaire (2020) multiple-scattering LUT; thick air (Venus, Titan) is too dark without it | Stand-in |
| `gpu-globe.ts` physical lighting | Ground hue from the survey palette, scaled to the kernel's albedo | No spectral reflectance per material yet | Spectral reflectance per material (light track) | Stand-in |
| `passes.ts` relief pass | The star's direction is a look setting (azimuth, elevation) and its light is white | The view has no ephemeris position at the ruler's date yet | Star direction from the orbit at the campaign date; star colour from its spectrum | Stand-in |
| `canvas.ts` air | One-column, plane-parallel air along view and sun; no limb glow beyond the disk | Canvas tier is the weak-device path | The GPU ray-march (already on GPU tiers) | Permanent for the canvas tier (weak devices do less) |
| `canvas.ts` exposure (R4) | Exposure is a plain brightness gain | The canvas tier has no tone mapping | Tone mapping on canvas (cost) | Permanent for the canvas tier |
| `renderer/src/star.ts` spotsFor (R5a) | Spot places and sizes drawn from the star's address and cycle number; belts follow Sporer's law linearly (28 to 8 degrees) | The kernel publishes spot coverage, not spot maps | A surface flux-transport model (active-region emergence, differential rotation, decay) as a kernel block | Stand-in |
| `star.ts` SPOT_DELTA_K | Every spot is one umbra 1700 K cooler with a simple penumbra | Berdyugina (2005) gives 1500 to 2000 K for Sun-like stars; one value for all | Spot contrast as a function of Teff (Berdyugina 2005 fig. 7) | Stand-in |
| `star.ts` CORONA_K | Corona at 1.5 MK for every star | No coronal temperature in the kernel yet | Coronal temperature from L_X (Güdel 2004 relation) published by read_activity | Stand-in |
| `star.ts` X-ray view | Active regions are Gaussian brightenings over spots; flares drawn at a spot; log emission-measure mapped to violet | X-ray images are mapped colour by nature; loops not modelled | Loop models (RTV scaling) over the spot map; flare sites from the kernel | Stand-in (the colour map is permanent and labelled) |
| `star.ts` limbDarkening | Grey atmosphere (Eddington) in every band | Physics-derived, no tables | Wavelength-dependent coefficients (Claret 2000 tables) by Teff and log g | To review |
| `star.ts` GRANULE_DT (O10 cells) | Granules are a temperature spread of 3.1% of Teff (rms) for every star, set from the Sun's measured 14% intensity contrast | The kernel publishes granule size, life and speed, not contrast | Contrast from the convective flux and Mach number (or simulation tables, e.g. Magic et al. 2013 Stagger grid) published by read_activity | Stand-in |
| `star.ts` granulesFor | Cell places (no longer drawn: the True view and the Body screen's star disc both use `star-churn.ts`): a jittered Fibonacci lattice per generation, a new set each granule lifetime (no motion within a life); cells keep their contrast up to the limb; supergranules and the network are not drawn | Places carry no physics (only size and count do); a few pixels per cell cannot show flows | Cells that drift and split over their life; contrast falling toward the limb from 3D models; network faculae near the limb | Stand-in |
| `weather.ts` withWeather (R2b) | Clouds and haze are laid on the ground samples as a reflectance (two-stream slab, adding method), not drawn at height: no cloud shadows, no parallax, no limb thickening, no absorption in the cloud | The six coarse field tiles (32 cells a face) are the weather's own detail; a cloud shell with its own shading is the R2b part 2 pass on the GPU tiers | A cloud shell at the layers' heights, ray-marched or billboarded with the same optical depth, shadowing the ground | Stand-in until R2b part 2 |
| `weather.ts` cloudReflectance | A layer's cover weights its slab reflectance (area mean); layers stack as if independent; one asymmetry parameter (0.85, water droplets) serves every condensate, ice and acid alike; clouds are grey (all colours alike) | The weather contract gives cover and optical depth per layer, not particle size, overlap or phase function | Overlap rules (maximum-random), g and single-scattering albedo by condensate and size from the matter sheet | To review |
| `weather.ts` haze | All aerosols in a cell are one slab of their summed optical depth (g = 0.7), tinted by the depth-weighted colour the weather block gives | The contract gives each aerosol's depth and colour, not its optical properties by wavelength | Per-aerosol scattering and absorption by wavelength (Mie tables per kind) | To review |
| `sky.ts` SKY_WHITE_MAG_ARCSEC2 and exposureFor (Y5) | The sky is shown with a fixed display exposure: 18 V mag per square arcsecond is full white. Other bands shift by the whole sky's brightness in them; they are not an eye or detector model | A screen cannot show the eye's dark adaptation or a sensor's response; one stated exposure keeps the bands comparable | An eye model (scotopic response, Purkinje shift) for V, and each instrument's response and exposure time from O8 sensors for the rest (Y6) | To review with Y6 |
| `sky.ts` pointSpread | Each star is a Gaussian of 0.8 px whose total light is its flux, scaled so the faintest chosen star just shows. Brighter stars look wider only because more of their spread clears one display level | The real point spread of an eye or telescope is far below a pixel at these fields | An instrument's own PSF and seeing from O8 and Y6 sensors, drawn at its true angular size when zoomed in | To review with Y6 |
| `sky.ts` glowSample | The HEALPix glow is blended between pixel centres with a Gaussian of 0.7 pixel spacing, in linear light | The kernel's map is coarse (nside 16, about 3.7° pixels) for speed; nearest-pixel drawing shows the pixels' diamonds | A finer map (nside 64) cached per vantage, when sky_glow is fast enough | Stand-in |
| `field.ts` compressed and inBubble (MF14a) | Inside the bubble the traced field is the body's own, plus one image of its dipole mirrored at the stand-off (Chapman and Ferraro 1931), plus the wind's field added uniformly (Dungey 1961, as Cowley 1973's vacuum superposition). No tail current sheet, no ring current, no plasma stretching; only the dipole part is mirrored; the mirror is a flat plane, so lines near the cusps and far down the night side can cross Shue's curved surface even with no wind field. `fieldFromRead` draws a published offset along the magnetic axis (its direction is not published yet) and takes the ecliptic as the spin equator | The Mag layer must show squeezing and open polar caps from the kernel's own numbers without a magnetospheric field model in the browser | A cited empirical model sized by the wind (Tsyganenko-style tail and ring currents, or Alexeev's paraboloid model), scaled to each body's stand-off | Stand-in |
| `field.ts` seeds and FIELD_LINES (MF14a) | Footpoints are spaced at equal steps of the dipole part's flux, so line spacing means strength for the dipole; a many-poled field is seeded the same way. Lower device tiers trace fewer lines | About 100 lines is what a desktop traces in under 10 ms; finer flux steps would be needed to show a small open cap | Seeding by the full field's flux through the surface (Br over the sphere), with the open cap seeded on its own | Stand-in |
| `ship.ts` HULL_SHAPE (item 14) | A hull is a cylinder four diameters long round its tanks, with a crew module one radius long and an engine bell 1.2 radii long; tanks are plain cylinders end to end, one per stored part of each fuel | Tank volume, shield mass and thrust come from the ship record; the proportions a designer would choose do not | Proportions as pack data per hull style, tanks with domed ends, and an engine sized from chamber pressure and nozzle expansion | Stand-in |
| `ship.ts` fuel shares and stand-in density (item 14) | Several fuels share the propellant mass equally (deuterium and helium-3, antimatter and hydrogen); a fuel the sheet cannot size is drawn at 100 kg m-3 and marked a stand-in | base-nav lists fuels without their mixture ratio, and the sheet has no stored densities for most of them yet | Mixture ratios in the ship record and `density_liquid` on the sheet for every fuel | Stand-in |
| `ship.ts` plumeShape (item 14) | The Chart plume is a 12° cone one hull radius long plus one radius per factor of ten of jet power above 1 MW | It is a glyph for the Chart style; a real vacuum plume is mostly invisible | True plumes from the exhaust's own emission (recombination, bremsstrahlung, annihilation) | Glyph |
| base-ships `drive_heat` (item 14, stage 2) | The waste heat a drive leaves in the ship is a fixed share of its jet power by energy source (0 for chemical and nuclear thermal while burning, a tenth for fusion and antimatter), radiated at 1500 K | No ship record carries its drive's waste heat yet, and the share depends on a drive design nobody has built | A `waste_per_jet` and radiator temperature on each ship record, from a drive model (neutron and bremsstrahlung fractions, gamma capture) | Rough |
| base-ships crew, cargo and `ship_styles` proportions (item 14, stage 2) | Quarters are 25 m³ of habitable volume a person at half the module's gross volume; cargo packs at 250 kg m-3; modules are cylinders; tanks cluster in rings; a style's ranges (tank fineness, drive gap, nose, radiator wing span over chord) are authored | They size and arrange the parts for pictures and play; the drive's numbers and the propellant's volume are the physics | Crew volume by mission length (the habitability curves), cargo by kind, and module shapes from pressure-vessel sizing | Stand-in |
| `gpu-ship.ts` skins and radiator glow (item 14, stage 3) | Each surface's albedo, roughness and metalness come from the style's `skin` (authored looks: white blankets, foil, bare truss, black radiators, gunmetal); panels are a tiled texture of 4 × 4 panels with seams and wear; hot radiators glow with the colour of a body at their temperature at a display brightness, not a radiance; one directional star and a flat fill light | A one-off model must read at a glance on every device; true radiance needs the photoreal exposure chain | Materials from the master data sheet's optical rows, radiators as blackbody emitters through the True exposure, and light from the scene's real star | Stand-in |
| base-ships `abilities` (item 14, stage 4) | Parts are sized by scaling one kernel answer by the law its figure follows (heating as 1/√r_n, stall as 1/√S) to 75% of the limit, in a few rounds; a shield is a blunt sphere-cone whose nose radius is 2.4 base radii (Apollo's), its area its base disc's, at most three hull radii across; material limits (tiles 0.5 MW/m², PICA 12 MW/m², carbon phenolic 300 MW/m²), ablation heats, wing and lifting-body lift coefficients, drag coefficients, leg touchdown (3 m/s) and parachute limits are rough figures to verify; a scoop fills the tanks in a day skimming air of 1e-5 kg/m³ at 50 km/s; legs, wheels and parachute canisters are placed, not designed; shield mass is reported inside the dry mass, not added | The designer must give the kernel numbers that look and behave like a ship that can do the act, and the kernel judges the act itself | Shield sizing from the full heat-load history and a material response model (thickness, char, recession), wings from a lift distribution, gear from the landing loads, and the shield and wing mass taken out of the dry mass budget | Rough, values to verify |
| Ship pass glow (item 14, stage 4b) | The shield face is drawn at radiative balance with the kernel's peak heating (no conduction, ablation or gas radiation), emissivity 0.85; the bow shock is a fixed paraboloid from Billig's sphere fit, drawn as additive glow at the colour of a body at 1.6 times the face temperature with opacity by heating; radiators are folded away rather than designed to retract | A picture of the pass that follows the kernel's numbers, not a flow solution | Shock shape from the Mach number and the gas, shock-layer radiation by species, the face temperature from the material response model | Display choice, physics rough |
| True system view (V10a) | Worlds are lit by their star with no fall-off across the frame (distances are drawn on a log scale), with a trace of ambient (0.04) so a night side does not vanish; sizes are not to scale; a world with no surface tiles (a giant, a sketched world) is drawn plain | A system seen whole cannot show true distances, sizes and brightness at once | Real brightness with an exposure chain, true sizes on request | Display choice |
| True star clock (V10c) | The True view's star runs on its own clock at six hours a second from the star's present, so its spin and flares are seen; each picture (ten a second) shows any flare that peaked since the last, however brief; flares sit over spots in turn (the kernel gives times, not places) | A star's day is weeks long and its flares last minutes: at the universe clock's pace nothing would be seen to move | The universe clock driving the star when it plays; flare places from the kernel | Display choice |
| `star-glow.ts` glare (SG1) | The glow round a star is the eye's own scatter, the CIE general disability glare equation (Vos and van den Berg 1999; age 25, pigment 0.5) summed over the drawn disc, used as the camera's; True is exposed so a white surface lit by the star reads white, which makes the glare π · PSF(θ) of white for every star (exposure 1); the even veil term (0.0025 p) is left out; the squeeze is L/(1 + L); the picture reaches 20°; the disc itself is drawn at its own exposure, so the glow is clear over it | Nothing glows round a star in space, the halo is in the eye or lens; the CIE equation is the published, cited one; a single exposure would blow the disc out white and hide its spots and cells | A lens point-spread function per camera as data (a photograph's glare is a lens's, not an eye's), the real flux at the eye where the view knows its distance (`exposure`), and one HDR exposure with a stated tone curve for disc and sky together | Display choice; stand-in for a lens PSF |
| `star-churn.ts` churningGranules (SG2) | One cell at a time on each home of a Fibonacci lattice; each home's clock is offset by a hashed share of a life; a cell's strength is sin(π · age / life), its dome at 35% of full when newborn or dying; it takes a fresh place up to half a spacing from home each life and drifts 0.3 of a spacing over it (`CELL_DRIFT`) | The kernel gives cell size, life and speed, not maps; this keeps the count and the life true and makes the surface boil smoothly | Cells that split, merge and push each other from a surface flow model (or 3D simulation snapshots such as the Stagger grid) | Stand-in |
| `star-churn.ts` granuleBlur (SG2) | A picture that stands for more than a cell's life shows the cells at 1/√N of their contrast (N lives averaged), as a long exposure would | At six hours a second the Sun's ten-minute granules would renew in every picture and boil as noise | Showing the true rate when the time stretch chip is pressed (exotic objects design 2.4) | Display choice |
| `star-churn.ts` limbLumps and `star.ts` LUMP_MAX (SG2) | The limb is lifted by the cell under it by one pressure scale height (the kernel's) per standard deviation of the pattern (`LUMP_SCALE_HEIGHTS` = 1), clipped at two deviations and at 0.04 of the radius per deviation so it fits the face's square | Freytag et al. 2002 and Chiavassa et al. 2010 show red supergiants' edges raised and lowered by their few giant cells; the scale height is the kernel's own number; the cap is the sprite's margin (the disc fills 0.92 of it) | The limb's height from 3D radiative hydrodynamics (CO5BOLD models) and an optical depth one surface that moves with the flow | Stand-in |
| `star-events.ts` shownFlares (SG3) | At most one flare begins in each third of a screen second (the largest; the rest are not shown), and each shown flare rises over at least a quarter and fades over at least a whole third of a second; under reduced motion no flare is shown and the words give the rate | The three-flashes-a-second rule (WCAG 2.3.1; exotic objects design 2.4) is a safety rule; the kernel's flares are minutes long, a fiftieth of a screen frame at six hours a second | None: this is a safety rule. The real rate shows when the clock runs slowly enough | Permanent (safety) |
| `star-events.ts` flare patches (SG3) | A flare's white-light patch is the kernel's 10,000 K area at its peak for the whole flare, brightening and fading with the light curve; drawn at least a pixel wide with its power spread to match; it adds black-body contrast per channel over the photosphere (limb darkening applied to it as to the disc) | The kernel's light description gives one temperature and area at the peak | A patch whose area grows and cools through the flare (ribbons), with its own limb behaviour | Stand-in |
| `star-events.ts` ejections (SG3) | An ejection is a thin shell (0.15 of its distance thick) inside its cone, its front at its speed from the flare's peak (no acceleration or drag); its light is Thomson scattering taken as even in all directions, σ_T N (R/r)² / 4 of the disc's, with N from the kernel's mass at 1.17 proton masses per electron; drawn as a coronagraph shows it, through a (r/R)² radial filter on a log scale from 10⁻¹¹ over three decades (`EJECTION_SCALE`) | To the eye an ejection is about a billion times fainter than its star and could never be seen beside it; a coronagraph's picture is the real way they are seen, and it is said beside the view | Thomson's (1 + cos² χ) angle law and the Van de Hulst coefficients, the three-part structure (front, cavity, core), drag on the wind | Display choice (scale), stand-in (shape) |
| `star-shells.ts` drawShells (SG5a) | A nebula round a star is drawn from its descriptor's shells (mass over a shell's volume, a linear taper across its thickness; a shell thicker than its radius as a 1/r² wind), lobes as a waist pinched by (1 + (ratio − 1) cos²θ), a jet as two cones of steady flow with Gaussian knots; light is density squared (gas) or density (dust) summed over 160 steps, optically thin, shown on a log scale three decades deep; line colours from Bruton's piecewise wavelength-to-RGB | The kernel publishes shells, lobes and jets as sizes and masses, not density maps; nebula photographs are always stretched on a log scale | Density and temperature fields from the light track's photoionisation and dust blocks, CIE colour of each line (the kernel's colour block, ND2), dust optical depth and scattering phase | Stand-in (shapes), display choice (scale) |
| `live-star.ts` spotsAt (VP-S4) | Every star's spots drift with the Sun's differential rotation shape (Snodgrass and Ulrich 1990) scaled to its own spin, from the star's present with no limit, though real spots live weeks | The kernel gives one spin period; the solar shape is measured and Barnes et al. 2005 find the shear changes only weakly with temperature | Shear by temperature and spin (Barnes 2005, Küker and Rüdiger), spots born and decaying from a flux-transport block | Stand-in |
| `star-corona.ts` (VP-S3) | The visible corona is Baumbach's solar fit for every star; its shape squeezes toward the equator by up to 80% at the cycle's quiet point (cos² by position angle, stronger with height) and is round at the peak; shown on a log scale three decades deep | The kernel gives the cycle's phase, not a coronal density model; the squeeze is read off eclipse pictures | A coronal density model from the star's field (potential-field source-surface) and its X-ray brightness | Stand-in (shape), display choice (scale) |
| Mag reach in Readable (V10e) | In the Readable scale each world's magnetosphere is drawn to its world's enlarged size, and its lines and bubble are cut off at 0.45 of the way to the nearest other body drawn | Readable enlarges worlds many times over, so a field drawn to that size would cover its neighbours and the star | Real scale, where every field is drawn at its true size and nothing is cut | Display choice |
| Ground below the fact floor (SL1) | Within 2 km of a landing site, ground finer than the kernel's spacing (76 m on Earth) is hashed value noise, four octaves from half the spacing down, as tall as the site's measured roughness (at most 5% of the spacing); off the kernel's samples it is bilinear between them | The kernel is exact to its fact floor and nothing finer exists (surface design 3.4: below the floor is decoration, never read by play) | GPU detail shaped by material and age (S4's amplification carried below the floor), and 10 m site tiles when the kernel takes a declared landing site | Display choice |
| Lava tube openings on the ground (TRUE6, renderer `cave-mouths.ts`) | Each fallen stretch of a kernel lava tube is cut into the walk patch (within 2 km of the site) as a straight trench of the kernel's width, its floor at the kernel's passage floor with no rubble; the broken roof edge is a wall drawn one twentieth of the width wide (at least 0.5 m) so a 10 m mesh shows it; beyond the walk patch the near ground's spacing is too coarse and the openings are named on the HUD only | The kernel places passages, widths and floors; it does not model the collapse's rubble cone or the rim's shape, and the near ground's grid is coarser than most skylights | Breakdown rubble on the floor (angle of repose from the material's data sheet row), overhangs at the rim, and the openings carried by the kernel's site tiles | Display choice |
| Surface stains in True (TRUE4, renderer `sheet-optics.ts`, `stain-look.ts`) | A stain's hue is a layer of its grains by Hapke's equivalent slab with no internal scatterers, at one grain size (1.5 µm for stains, 50 µm for bare grains), in its star's blackbody light with the eye adapted to it; the hue is laid over the ground's existing colour at the kernel's albedo, by the stain's share, the same everywhere on the world | The kernel gives a stain's share per world, not per tile, and no grain sizes | Per-tile stains (wind-blown dust in basins, tholin by latitude), grain sizes from the kernel's regolith, full Hapke bidirectional reflectance with the opposition surge | Stand-in |
| Sky at the ground (SL1) | The sky is single Rayleigh scattering in closed form, with the air mass of a uniform shell one scale height thick; no multiple scattering, aerosols or clouds; the air above the eye is the zenith depth thinned by the eye's height; light from the sky on the ground is half of what the sun's beam loses on its way down; a fixed exposure (6) before the shared finish's AgX tone mapping (VP1) | One closed form per pixel runs on any GPU and uses only the kernel's optics; skies read darker and bluer than real ones | Hillaire (2020) multiple scattering and aerosols, as on the globe's follow-up, and exposure from the scene's light | Display choice |
| Main gas as the whole air | A world with no gas mix on record (the catalogue and the kernel give `atmosphere.main` only) has its air's optics read as if all of it were that main gas: Earth's sky as pure nitrogen | Now only for worlds with no mix on record: since the data sheet's PR 336 the catalogued Earth, Venus, Mars, Pluto and the four giants carry one `atmosphere.composition.<gas>` fact per gas (the giants at 1 bar), read in preference; nitrogen alone scatters within a few per cent of Earth's real mix | Mixes for the other worlds as the sheet gains them | Missing data |
| Rings in True (V10f) | A world's rings are a flat annulus in its equator, each pixel's cover 1 − e^(−τ) as seen face on and its colour the world's mean surface colour times the albedo. They are lit as a layer of particles scattering once with an isotropic phase function (I/F ∝ μ₀/(μ₀ + μ), Chandrasekhar 1960); seen from the unlit face they shine at a quarter of the lit face's light, not by computed diffuse transmission. The cover is not thickened by the slant of the line of sight (1/μ). The world's shadow on its rings and theirs on it are drawn since EC1 (gpu-shade.ts). A ring with no τ or albedo on record is drawn at τ 0.5, albedo 0.5. The pole leans toward +x of the frame, since only the tilt is on record | Rings read right at a glance from the ring data alone; the slant, the shadow and transmission need a shader of their own | Slant-thickened cover, the world's shadow, diffuse transmission by τ and the particles' measured phase function (Cuzzi et al. 1984) in a ring shader; ring colours from the data sheet; the pole's direction when the catalogue carries it | Display choice |
| Belts in True (V10f) | A belt round the star is 2,400 points of a neutral grey between its edges, each a member on its own circular orbit (spread evenly by area, at random angles, tilts from a Rayleigh spread of 0.08 rad, drawn by a hash of its number), going round at the Kepler rate on the belts' own clock of thirty days a second; the count, the brightness and the clock are a drawing's, not the belt's (a real belt is invisible to the eye at this scale), and the worlds do not move with it | Without it a belt is not shown at all in True; with no motion it reads as a pattern, not a belt | Points from the belt's real largest members (F5's size spread) and their own eccentricities and tilts, on the universe clock with the worlds | Display choice |
| Air in True (R1 to R3, R6; `packages/renderer/src/true-air.ts`) | A world's clouds and haze in system True are the weather op's field (six faces, 24 cells across each), each pixel a Gaussian blend of the cells within three half-cell widths. Each deck is a two-stream slab of its cover and optical depth (g 0.85), tinted by its condensate's `condensed_colour_rgb` on the sheet (itself authored, F-register) and drawn white when the sheet has none (NH4SH, H2SO4, silicates); the haze is one slab of the summed aerosol depth (g 0.7) in the weather op's display colour (pack-typed, e.g. Titan's [0.85, 0.55, 0.25]); slabs stack by the adding method with no absorption. Below a giant's lowest deck the light is taken as lost (black floor). No gas above the decks yet | Giants show their bands, storms and polar cyclones and rocky worlds their clouds, from kernel numbers, before the colour rows exist | Deck and haze colours from the sheet's refractive indices through `sse.light.particle_albedo` and `sse.light.slab_reflectance` (MP33 rows, the giants' chromophore haze W10a); methane and Rayleigh scattering above the decks through `light_through_gas`; the deep atmosphere's own scattering; the haze at its height (W10) | Stand-in until the rows land |
| Giants carried by their winds (VP-A1; `packages/renderer/src/true-air.ts` `flowFace`) | A giant's face slides row by row at its jet's speed, u / (a cos φ), with u the mean eastward wind of the highest showing deck in that row of the weather op's cells; a storm from the `systems` list moves as one piece at its centre's speed within a Gaussian of its radius (half its size, as the weather block's own storm winds); it turns at the angular speed of the cells' winds round its centre, drawn in cycles of a quarter turn at its edge, two phases blended (the usual flow-map method) so the faster core does not wind into a spiral. The face is not re-read from the kernel while it slides, so storms born or ended since are not shown until it is | Bands drift and storms turn by the kernel's own winds, at no cost, before any eddy is resolved | Cloud tracers advected through the weather block's own wind field, the storms' shapes from a resolved vortex (VP-A7) | Stand-in for the visible motion; every speed is the kernel's |
| Air's rim in True (VP-A3; `packages/renderer/src/air-luts.ts`, `gpu-air-shell.ts`) | A world's air is a shell to ten scale heights, drawn with Hillaire's (2020) transmittance and multiple-scattering look-up tables built from the kernel's `read_air_optics` (Rayleigh at the ground and the scale height). Rayleigh only: no haze, dust or droplets in the shell yet; no light bounced off the ground into the air; the tables are indexed linearly in height and zenith cosine (Hillaire's horizon-weighted mapping not used), 64 × 32 and 32 × 32, and the view ray takes 16 steps; the sun's strength is the scene's point light (3) | A real rim and a soft blue terminator on every world with air, from kernel numbers, at a few texture reads a pixel | Aerosol and cloud extinction in the shell from the weather op and the sheet's particle optics (VP-A5, K-e); ground albedo in the multiple-scattering table; Hillaire's non-linear mapping and the aerial-perspective volume | Stand-in until the hazes are in |
| Haze that absorbs, and a giant's deep air (TA3a, TA4; `packages/renderer/src/true-air.ts` `addAbsorbing`, `albedoOfColour`, `deepAirOf`, `CHROMOPHORE_VISIBLE`) | Each weather aerosol's colour is read as how a thick layer of it looks; its single-scattering albedo per channel is that colour through the semi-infinite two-stream reflectance inverted, w = 1 - ((1 - R) / (1 + R))^2 (Bohren 1987), so it absorbs τ (1 - w) per channel and diffuse light crosses it with a diffusivity factor 1.66 each way (Elsasser; Goody and Yung 1989). The chromophore's colour is still the climate thread's stand-in, and its optical depth, given at 1 µm (Sromovsky et al. 2017), is carried to the visible by an Ångström exponent of 1.5, a stand-in for its particle optics. A giant's deep air below its decks is a semi-infinite clear gas: per wavelength, Rayleigh scattering over scattering plus its gases' absorption (both from the kernel), two-stream reflectance, then each channel the plain mean over a few wavelengths (600 to 700, 500 to 600, 420 to 500 nm) rather than the eye's colour-matching weights; the gas over the decks is one scale-height layer from the 1 bar level. In the clouds-only layer (VP-A4) the ground under a haze is dimmed by one alpha, not by colour | Jupiter's belts tan and Saturn butterscotch from the kernel's own chromophore depths, Uranus and Neptune cyan and blue from methane, Titan's haze takes the blue out | Each aerosol's absorption and its depth across wavelengths from its refractive index on the data sheet, through TA2's particle block (needs a chromophore row). The deep air's spectrum through the eye's colour-matching functions. Decks at their own pressures with the gas between them (VP-A4 part 2). Coloured transmittance through the clouds layer | Stand-in until the sheet has the chromophore's optics |
| Clouds as their own layer (VP-A4 part 1; `true-air.ts` `withAir`, `gpu.ts` `clouds`) | A world's clouds and haze are drawn as their own lit, premultiplied layer over its bare ground (so the ground keeps its relief), a thousandth of the radius above it: the weather op gives no deck heights yet. Laying the layer over the ground (C + (1 - α) G) differs a little from the adding method's R + T² G / (1 - R G) used for the flat face | Clouds over relief, at no cost | Each deck at its own height from the weather op's `base_m` and `top_m` (agreed with the weather thread 24 Nov, after PR 418), with shadows offset by the sun's angle | Stand-in until the heights land |
| Worlds flattened by spin (TA6; `packages/renderer/src/true-shape.ts`, flow `sse.base.body-shape`) | The flattening is the Darwin-Radau relation on the world's moment of inertia factor, which holds for a body near hydrostatic equilibrium turning slowly (q small); the factor itself is the proven core's computed one until base-sol carries measured values (Saturn's computed 0.230 sits above the models' about 0.21, so Saturn comes out a little round). The air's shell and the rim stay spheres of the equatorial radius | Jupiter and Saturn visibly flattened from their own facts, no typed shape | A rotating-polytrope or concentric-Maclaurin-spheroid shape (Hubbard 2013) for fast spinners; the shell following the flattened surface | Stand-in until the measured inertia factors and a fast-spinner block |
| Station sizing rules (ST-gen) | A station's working space is 50 m³ a head (the ship rule) and a home 6,900 m³ a head (the Stanford torus); power is 10 kW a head unless the record gives it, from solar panels at 30% and radiators at 300 K; a berth for every 500 people, doubled at a port, each class up half as many, the biggest taking ships to half the station's size; listed modules and a few more per decade of people are bolt-ons of 2% of the volume; mass is 150 kg per m³ lived in unless the record gives it; a town on the ground spreads at 4,000 people a km² | A picture sized from the record with no per-station hand work | Rules from a settlement design study per kind of place, traffic from the 4.1 economy, power from the construct's own plant (a fusion port needs radiators, not solar panels) | base-ships station_design, authored |
| Station megastructure line (ST-gen) | A spun hull needs strength over density of g R, doubled for the air, soil and shielding it carries and doubled again for safety; past base-tech's best built material (steel wire) it is a megastructure, past the carbon bond fictional. A tower stands to strength over density over g, halved for safety. A construct whose escape speed passes 1 m/s is a body, not a building | The line where a station stops being a building is read off the physics, not set by hand | A hull of the chosen material sized for its pressure and load (thin-shell membrane stress plus spin), and the kernel treating such a mass as a body | base-ships station_design (hoop, tower, self_gravity), authored safety factors |
| Station shapes (ST-gen) | About two dozen shapes, each a fixed arrangement with set proportions (a ring's tube is 8% of its radius, a cylinder five radii long, a rock a third rooms) picked from the record's words; berths drawn up to 12 a class | Recognisable stations at any size from a line of description | Shapes from the history of station designs with their own engineering, and a by-hand builder in 4.2 | Display choice |
| Turning globes in the size comparison (V14g) | Each globe's pole leans sideways across the view by its tilt (which way it leans is not on record, as for rings), its spin phase starts at zero when the globes appear, and it is sped up: every body one turn in 40 s (Same pace), or the quickest in the lineup one turn in 40 s and the rest in proportion (True pace). A body whose day is not on record stands still. A world whose surface the kernel cannot read is a plain grey in True, as in the system's 3D; a reference world's rings take a neutral grey | Real turning would be too slow to see (Venus takes 243 days); the pole's direction and the prime meridian's angle at an epoch are not on record | Pole directions and prime-meridian angles at an epoch (IAU WGCCRE) from the data sheet for catalogued worlds, from formation for made ones; colours for reference worlds from their measured albedo spectra | Display choice |
| The sun's clock on the ground (Land part 2) | On the Land screen the star's hour angle comes from the world's spin and year (one solar day is 1 / abs(1/P_spin − 1/P_orbit)) and its declination from the tilt and the world's place on its orbit, but the spin's phase at the universe's time 0 is taken as noon at longitude 0, and the pole is taken to lean toward the frame's reference direction (the same choice as the rings' pole) | Neither a world's rotation phase nor its pole's direction is on record: the catalogue and the kernel give the period and the obliquity only | A pole direction (right ascension and declination of the north pole, IAU WGCCRE) and a prime-meridian angle at an epoch, from the data sheet, for the catalogued worlds; for made worlds, from formation | Missing data |
| Stars over the site at night (SL-night) | The kernel's sky from the system (stars to magnitude 6.5) is turned onto the site's sky round the world's pole, which leans toward the frame's reference direction by its tilt (the same choice as the sun's clock on the ground); each star's brightness is its bolometric light over the home star's at the world (L / d² against L_host / a²), coloured by its temperature, dimmed by the air along its line of sight. A system with no place in the galaxy yet sees the Sun's sky, laid square on its frame | The pole's direction is not on record; a band-weighted (visual) share would need each star's spectrum through the eye's response | Pole directions from the data sheet (IAU WGCCRE) and each star's visual flux from its spectrum | Missing data |
| The eye's adaptation on the ground (SL-night) | The exposure follows the light about the eye: the day's exposure times how much darker the zenith sky plus sunlit ground (albedo 0.1) is than with the sun 45° up, capped at 10⁵ (about a dark-adapted eye's gain) | A real eye's adaptation takes minutes and shifts toward blue (Purkinje); the ground's albedo is not read from the site | The site's own albedo, and a time course for adaptation | Simplification |
| The eye's glare in the shared finish (VP1) | The picture convolved with the CIE glare equation is drawn as up to six Gaussian blurs whose widths double, each carrying the share the equation scatters into its ring of angles (from 0.1° or one pixel out to 20°); the exposure is fixed at 1 (a white surface in the star's daylight) rather than metered | A full-resolution convolution with the equation's kernel costs far too much per frame; metering waits on the tiers builder's governor wiring | An FFT convolution with the true kernel at rest (sharpen at rest, VP5), and exposure metered on the view's centre with slow adaptation | Simplification |
| The sky's stars behind True (VP2) | The kernel's stars to magnitude 6.5 are drawn one pixel each behind the system's True, at π times their bolometric flux over the Sun's at 1 au per pixel's solid angle (True's white is a surface under the solar constant, whatever the world in view). A system with no place in the galaxy yet sees the Sun's sky, laid square on its frame. Long exposure, the default (Alex, 24 Nov), lifts them until the 40th brightest fills its pixel and clips each at four times white | Visual flux needs each star's spectrum through the eye's response; a star's light spread by the eye and the optics over its few pixels is the finish's glare, not modelled per star; True's exposure is not yet metered on the world in view | V-band fluxes from the kernel's magnitudes, the system frame on every system, and metered exposure (VP4) | Simplification |
| The close-up's floor and tilt (True, Alex 25 Nov) | In True the camera comes down to 3 km over the world at the centre of the frame, or 0.02% of its radius if that is more (14 km over Jupiter); within three radii it looks progressively toward the horizon (aimed 0.3 fields of view short of it at the floor), and its ground is the world's tiles as read so far, smooth under them until the detail ladder's deeper tiles and roughness (VP3 part 4) arrive. The world does not turn under the camera in this view | A real camera could land; the floor keeps the horizon's curve and the near plane workable. The world's spin in the system view waits on its rotation phase (the fudge row on the sun's clock on the ground) | Land (the Surface screen) for anything lower; spin from a recorded prime meridian | Display choice |
| The sea drawn on the ground (V10k) | The kernel's sea state at the site (significant height and peak period, `sse.ocean.wind_waves`) is drawn as up to six long-crested wave trains: the kernel's own when it sends them (W-sea3: wavelength, amplitude, period and heading from the liquid's capillary-gravity dispersion; their starting phases spread by the golden angle, since the kernel gives a spectrum, not crest positions), otherwise four deep-water trains round the wind's direction (wavelengths 0.4 to 1.5 times the peak's, ω² = g k, variance summing to (Hs/4)²); froth (W-sea4) covers the kernel's whitecap share as soft streaks along the wind in 1 by 6 m cells chosen by a hash (how much is the kernel's, where is a picture choice; shares above 35% saturate), the sky mirrored by Schlick's water-like Fresnel term until the kernel sends the liquid's refractive index (W-sea2: then the exact unpolarised Fresnel split at that index), the water's body the ground colour under the sea until the kernel sends the liquid's deep reflectance and absorption (then the deep colour and the floor seen through the liquid, faded by exp(−2 a z) with the diffuse path taken as the depth), and the sun's glint as a narrow lobe; the tides' raisers are all taken in phase at the universe's time 0 | A full directional spectrum (JONSWAP with a spreading function), whitecaps and the glint's true width from the unresolved slopes (Cox and Munk 1954) are more than a ground view needs yet; the tides' phases are not on record | A spectrum sampled from the kernel's figures, Cox–Munk glint, and the tide's phase from the raisers' places | Simplification |
| The sun's place over a site (SL1) | The star stands where it would at the equinox (declination 0) at the hour on the Time of day slider | The world's spin, tilt and the campaign's date are not yet read into the view | The sun's direction from the world's rotation, axial tilt and orbit at the universe clock (the Sky from here screen's frame) | To replace |
| The flown descent's ground track (SL1) | The kernel's descent has a downrange distance but no heading, so it is laid in from the west (as a prograde entry would come) and ends on the site; it is drawn from where the far ground is in sight (102 km out), and played at a steady apparent motion of the ground, not at the kernel's pace | A heading needs the arrival orbit; the far ground is read only 120 km round the site | The heading from the arrival orbit (Voyages' approach), and the world's globe beyond the far ground | Display choice |
| Where liquid sits (SL1) | Ground under a liquid cover is drawn with its surface at the datum (sea level), or at its own floor when that is higher (a highland lake shows its bed) | Tiles give the cover but not the liquid's level, and heights are measured from the world's sea level | The liquid level per basin (sse-surface `Flood.level_m`) in the tile | To replace |
| True galaxy shapes (V9t) | Between the kernel's ring-by-ring numbers, the True galaxy's shapes are stand-ins. Each arm crest is a Gaussian 0.18 disc scale lengths wide, with 3 times the gas on its crest at the chart's arm contrast. The dust lane sits 0.12 scale lengths inside the crest. Clouds are three octaves of value noise. The bar runs from the centre to where the arms start, 0.3 of that wide. The bulge is a Gaussian glow 0.2 scale lengths across. The disc is seven sech² layers 0.3 kpc thick | The kernel gives each ring's stars and gas, the arm crests, the bulge mass and a barred flag, but not an arm's width, the clouds, the bar's shape or the bulge's size | Arm widths, the bar's length and the bulge's size from the kernel (galaxy design G3) | Display choice |
| Real scale points (V10a) | In Real scale a body smaller than about a pixel (a star 1.5, a world 0.75 pixels in radius at its distance from the camera) is drawn at that size, so it shows as a point of light; Real's locator rings with HUD on | A true-size world far smaller than a pixel would vanish, which no eye or camera sees: a star or lit world shows as a point | Brightness from flux and an exposure chain, so a point's brightness is its real one | Display choice |
| `galaxy-marks.ts` SCAR_SHAPES (GH9) | Each mark is a formula over the disc: a warp lifting the edge by up to 0.15 of the radius past 3.5 scale lengths; ripples up to 0.3 kpc high, 1.5 scale lengths apart, wound up to three times tighter as they age; an outer tilt up to 10°; a ring expanding at 70 km/s with an emptied middle; lopsided and two-armed brightness up to 0.35 and 0.6; thin and thick discs 0.3 and 0.9 kpc high. The strongest live mark of a kind sets that kind's size and direction; ripples share a union. Directions come from the culprit's address, since the record keeps none | The kernel's marks carry strength, age and a few shape numbers; the disc's real response to a pass is not modelled | The disc's response from the encounter itself (impulse approximation for warps and ripples, Lynds and Toomre's ring kinematics), each mark's direction from the satellite's orbit, and the True style drawing the stars and gas themselves | Stand-in |


### 3. Kernel light track (light thread, PR 24)

| Where | Instead of the physics | Why | Real physics to replace it | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| crates/sse-light emit (O2) | Grey Bond albedo and a black body at the effective temperature for planets; no bands, no day-night contrast | atmosphere model not built | spectral albedo and emission from the atmosphere model | stand-in |
| sse.light.stellar_wind (O2) | Every star gets the Sun's wind at 1 au (Parker), scaled by distance | per-star winds came with O10 | wind strength from O10 activity | stand-in, now replaceable |
| emit auroral radio (O2) | Isotropic and flat in frequency from f_max/30 to f_max | simple, energy-correct | beamed emission cones, rotation modulation | stand-in |
| emit corona (O2) | Coronal X-rays flat over 0.1 to 10 keV | no coronal temperature model | thermal plasma emission at the coronal temperature | stand-in |
| emit lamps (O2) | White lamps flat over 400 to 700 nm | no lamp spectra table | lamp spectra per technology as pack data | stand-in |
| emit accretion disc (O1) | Thin disc seen isotropically, no inclination or limb effects | view angle not known in the description | inclination-dependent disc emission | stand-in |
| sse-nebula (O3) | Planetary nebula speeds, shell share, pulse timing and ring count drawn from stated ranges; core at 100 000 K | published spreads, no hydrodynamic model | radiation-hydrodynamic PN models | stand-in |
| sse-activity (O10) spin from age | Skumanich P ∝ √t scaled by turnover time, never faster than a 1-day birth spin | the life line has no spin yet | spin from the life line (F7) or a torque model (Matt et al. 2015) | stand-in |
| sse-activity tides | Tidal lock assumed below a 10-day companion orbit | no tidal synchronisation model | Zahn-type synchronisation time | stand-in |
| sse-activity regimes | Cut-offs at 10 000 K (hot wind), 6900 K (no corona), 0.05 R☉ (compact); labels at 1e-4 and 3e-7 L_X/L_bol | conditions, not kinds; labels only | convective envelope depth from structure models | stand-in |
| sse-activity spots and cycle | Spot cover ∝ √(L_X/L_bol) from the Sun's 0.3 %, cap 50 %; cycle a sine of amplitude 0.7, branch at 1e-5 | no flux-transport model | surface flux transport model | stand-in |
| sse-activity flares | Durations ∝ E^0.39 from 600 s at 1e25 J; light a 10 000 K black body plus 10 % soft X-rays; largest 1e29 J | Kepler-era statistics only | flare spectra and duration statistics from TESS/Kepler studies | stand-in |
| sse-activity ejections | Share, speed and mass by power laws in flare energy; cone half-width 30°, within 30° of the equator; planets circular in the equator | no CME model | CME catalogues (Yashiro) and an ephemeris | stand-in |
| sse_light::observe (O12) absorption | Qualitative 0-to-1 line strengths by temperature, same for every metal content; no line widths or reddening | O4 grid not built | line-strength grid (results-as-data), broadening, dust | stand-in until O4 |
| sse-survey (O13) | White noise ∝ 1/√N, no spots or flares in light curves; activity 15 ppm/hr, jitter 1 m/s, efficiency 0.5, IWA 3λ/D; found at S/N 7.1 with 3 transits; eccentric transits timed as circular; no microlensing | closed-form, cheap | red-noise and activity models (O10), full light-curve simulation | stand-in |
| sse-sense (O9) | One dust power law (λ/0.55 µm)^−1.75 from 0.3 µm to 1 mm; all-or-nothing glare inside the resolution; photon energy at band centre; radar listener in the main beam | closed-form | an extinction curve (Cardelli, Draine), PSF halo, sidelobes | stand-in |
| sse_light::air | Other gases follow air's dispersion scaled to 589 nm; King factors constant except N₂ and O₂; isothermal scale height; gravity at the surface; Rayleigh only | small table | per-gas dispersion (Sneep and Ubachs 2005), haze, absorption | stand-in |
| sse_light::air gases NH₃, SO₂, CO, Ne | King factor 1 (no depolarisation); refractivity values from memory of Kaye and Laby | a few per cent on σ (SO₂ more) | tabulated depolarisation ratios and a checked source | stand-in |
| sse_field (MF6 part 1) | Air boundary where an isothermal upper atmosphere's pressure equals the outside pressure (no ionosphere profile); Jupiter's bubble from its dipole alone (no plasma inflation, so about 40 radii, not 60 to 90); induction from the thin-shell response with thickness capped at the skin depth, at one frequency; wind gusts as a fixed 0.25 to 4 times ram pressure | Venus boundary 566 km against 250 to 400 km; Jupiter nose half the measured | ionosphere profile from photochemistry; plasma sources and pressure (MF7); full spherical-shell induction at several frequencies; gusts from O10's wind variability | stand-in |
| sse.aurora.* (MF9 part 1) | Wind oval is the footprint of the dipole line through the bubble's nose (no open-flux budget, no ring current, no stretched night side); spin oval at the dipole Hill distance with plasma loading and conductance handed in; a tilted centred dipole for the spin-latitude range; no power and no colours yet | Earth 71.6° against the oval's 67° to 75°; Jupiter's main oval maps to 27 R_J (measured 20 to 30), Io's spot 65.7° in dipole terms | open flux from the wind's reconnection rate (Newell et al. 2007, Milan 2004); current-sheet field (Nichols 2011 eq. 39); oval power as a share of the wind's power; colours from the air's own lines; storm events from O10's ejections | stand-in |
| Aurora stand-in, renderer `aurora.ts` (VP-AU5 part 1) | Until MF9 part 2 gives each line's power, height and thickness: oxygen's 557.7 nm and 630.0 nm lines only, at Earth's measured peak heights (110 km and 250 km) and half-peak thicknesses (30 km and 100 km) times the air's surface scale height over Earth's 8.5 km; a fixed 10 kR green and 2 kR red; a 5° oval width; drawn only where O₂ or O is at least 1% of the air, so no other world borrows Earth's colours. Magnetic pole longitude drawn at the field's reference longitude (not published). Moon spots placed by the kernel but not drawn. Colour vision fading taken as 0.001 to 0.01 cd/m² (mesopic range, to check against CIE 191) | the drawing can be built and proven before the facts land; it switches itself off per oval the moment the kernel's `emissions` arrive | MF9 part 2 (light thread): power per driver, each line's height from stopping depth and quenching against the air's own density profile, its thickness and colour; pole longitudes (F41); moon positions from the system | stand-in |
| Aurora shells, renderer `gpu-aurora.ts` (VP-AU5 part 1) | Each line one thin spherical shell at its peak height with a Gaussian ring across the oval (full width as the half-peak width), symmetric about both magnetic poles; path length 1/cos of the view angle capped at the tangent chord √(2r/t); no curtain structure, no light from the air below dimming it; daylight drowns a shell point over a fixed band of the terminator (cos of the sun angle from 0.05 to −0.12) | cheap (one shell per line), and the limb stacking it gives is real | the vertical emission profile per line as a volume (VP-AU8), curtains through the detail ladder (VP-AU6), the air's own brightness on the day and twilight side from the atmosphere pass | stand-in |
| Aurora exposure, temporary, renderer `aurora.ts` `exposeAurora` (VP-AU5 part 1) | The aurora pass does its own long-exposure lift (the brightest line to 0.6 of an albedo-0.3 sunlit ground, one ratio for all lines) and its own Eye greying, before the globe's ACES tone map | VP1's shared finish is not built yet, and "Long exposure" is the default (Alex, 24 Nov 19:12) | VP1 `finish.ts`: the shells emit real radiance into the shared half-float target, and the finish's metered exposure, lift ratio and glare apply to every faint layer at once | temporary |
| Aurora from the ground, renderer `aurora.ts` `auroraSky` and `gpu-aurora-sky.ts` (VP-AU7) | Each shell seen from the site as a thin layer: the sight line meets it once, brightness is the ring value times the slant path (capped at the tangent chord), dimmed by the air below along the airmass with the same optical depth the sky pass uses; no curtain structure, no scattering of aurora light by the air. Long exposure lift brings the brightest point of the whole sky (sampled every 6° in elevation and 15° in azimuth) to 0.35 of display white after the site's own metered exposure | the arc low on the horizon is up to forty times brighter than overhead, so a face-on lift saturates the sky; the sky sample keeps the arc's real shape and ratios | VP1 `finish.ts` metering the aurora with every other faint layer; curtains (VP-AU6) | temporary |
| sse_light::photometry (Y2 light side) | A fill star is a blackbody photosphere; each band is read at its effective wavelength (no filter curve), so a band's magnitude is the monochromatic AB magnitude there; dust's dimming per band is the CCM89 curve at the same wavelength | Sun M_V 4.85 against 4.78, B−V 0.65 against 0.65, V−Ks 1.46 against 1.49; a 10,000 K blackbody reads B−V 0.14 where filter-integrated photometry gives about 0 (Ballesteros 2012) | filter transmission curves from the pack (Bessell 2012, Cohen 2003) integrated over the full spectrum; model atmospheres (Balmer jump, molecular bands of cool stars) in place of the blackbody for fill stars | stand-in |
| sse.shield.* (MF7 part 1, cosmic-ray dose) | Vertical Størmer cut-off of a centred dipole (no tilt, offset or wind compression; own field cut off beyond its stand-off); free-space dose one measured number (1.84 mSv a day, MSL cruise near 1 AU) for every star and galaxy place; space share (1 + R/2 GV)^−1.7 with a turnover standing in for the star's modulation; ground share the sea-level Dorman function (α 10, k 1, recalled from Clem and Dorman 2000, to verify) at every depth, blended with the space share by the nucleonic absorption; two-part air (140 g/cm² nucleonic, 600 g/cm² muons) with the muon share 0.0045 fitted to UNSCEAR's sea-level dose; no Pfotzer peak; the body blocks half the sky on the ground; a moon in a host's bubble takes the host's equatorial cut-off at its orbit | Earth sea level 0.35 mSv a year against 0.39 (muon share fitted); Mars surface 0.82 mSv a day against 0.64 (Hassler 2014); Moon 0.92 against 1.37 (Zhang 2020, which includes albedo neutrons); equatorial cut-off 14.8 GV against 14.9 | free-space dose from the star's wind (heliosphere size, Potgieter 2013) and the galaxy place; yield functions by depth (Mishev, Usoskin) as a table; tilted and offset fields from MF3; the star's particle storms and belts (MF7 part 2) | stand-in |
| sse.convection.* (star surface cells) | Granule = 10 pressure scale heights, life = half a crossing time, supergranule = 25 granules with flows a fifth of the granule speed, convective speed ∝ T^(5/3) g^(−1/3) from flux with fixed photospheric opacity, all fitted on the Sun; one mean molecular weight 1.3 | Sun matched by construction; Betelgeuse a handful of large cells as simulated; cool white dwarf cells 0.4 km against about 1 km and speeds far too low (Tremblay et al. 2013) | 3D-model grid (Trampedach 2013, Magic 2013 Stagger grid) as tabulated results | stand-in |
| sse_cosmos::patch glows | Expected nebulae as steady-state counts (rate × visible life); one mean ambient density (1 H per cm³) for every patch; planetary nebula shell at mid-range speed; ionising stars (≥ 15 M☉) stand in for H II regions, which group many such stars; main-sequence life 10 Gyr × M^−2.5 | counts within a factor of two or three near the Sun | local gas density from the patch, cluster grouping of ionising stars | stand-in |

## Master data sheet (`packs/base-reference`)

Sheet rows whose `src` is `fudge-register`: a stand-in value kept until a source is read.

| Where | Instead of the physics | Why | Real physics to replace it | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `substances[he3].phases.density_liquid` (MP23) | 59 kg m-3 at the 3.19 K boiling point, recalled, not read | Liquid helium-3 has no equation of state in CoolProp, and the sources read so far stop below 2.5 K (Kollar and Vollhardt, arXiv cond-mat/9906222) or give no density (Wikipedia's liquid helium table) | The saturated-liquid density of helium-3 from a read source (Wheatley 1975, Rev. Mod. Phys. 47, 415, or a helium-3 equation of state such as Huang et al.) | stand-in; ship tanks only |

## Real spaceflight (track RS, `tests/fixtures/flightlog/`, `store-packs/sol-today/logs/`)

| Where | Instead of the physics | Why | Real physics to replace it | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Every real craft's flight log (big burns only) | Only big burns and flybys are logged; trim manoeuvres, station-keeping and drag are left out, and orbits between events are held fixed. Each course is solved to meet its next recorded event, so a craft is right at every logged event and off only in between | Alex's ruling, 23 Nov 2026: "we simplify and approximate... just the big burns and assume stable orbits" | A reconstructed ephemeris (JPL Horizons or SPICE) for each craft. Estimated error: mid-leg thousands to tens of thousands of km on legs of hundreds of millions in deep space (well under 0.1%); in low orbit the phase along the orbit drifts, good only to a fraction of an orbit after a few days. Measured 24 Nov 2026 (RS3, `crates/sse-nav/tests/sol_today_logs.rs`): Cassini's log against Horizons at seven cruise dates is off by 2 thousand to 8 million km, at most 1.4% of its distance from the Sun (worst mid-way from Earth to Jupiter), the arcs being solved to the planets' centres on the NAV fixture's own planet orbits. In low orbit (the ISS log, one Horizons orbit a year): 41 km off an hour after a sample, 200 km after 6 hours, 720 km after a day, 5,000 km after a week and 12,900 km (the far side of Earth) after a month, as drag and the Earth's oblateness move the phase; so low-orbit logs need a sample every few hours to be right in place, and are right in size and plane between. Ion-driven Dawn and JWST near L2 are sampled every few months for the same reason. Cassini's thirteen years at Saturn are one osculating orbit per year from Horizons: the Titan flybys that reshape it fall below the line | accepted fudge, measured in cruise |
| `voyager-2.json` departure burn | One impulsive 7.19 km/s burn an hour after launch from a 185 km parking orbit, in place of the Centaur's two burns and the solid kick stage | Gives the right speed leaving Earth (Horizons, 10.15 km/s a day after launch) with one event | The Centaur and TE-M-364-4 burns with their own times and engines (RS2 parts, RS5 ascent) | stand-in |
| `logs/cassini.json` Saturn orbit insertion | 576 m/s impulsive, from Horizons' elements before and after the 96 min burn; 576 to 645 m/s by the window chosen, as Saturn's oblateness moves osculating elements near periapsis | One event, honest to Horizons | The burn's own figure from the Cassini navigation papers | stand-in |
| Huygens' landing time and site | Horizons' predicted descent end (12:07 TDB, 14 Jan 2005) and a site from memory | The flown descent is not in Horizons | ESA's descent reconstruction | stand-in |
| STS-1 burns and times | The press kit's pre-flight plan; the SRB splashdowns and the tank's breakup are estimated | The flown times are in the mission report, not read yet | The STS-1 mission report | stand-in |
| Inclinations in `orbit()` pieces (Apollo 11, STS-1, Falcon 9, SNAP-10A, parking orbits) | The published inclination, to the equator, is turned to the ecliptic with the node put at the equinox (RS8 part 3, 24 Nov 2026), as the node is not known | The logs were written from mission sizes; Horizons-sampled logs (Cassini, Juno, Dawn, MSL, JWST, ISS) carry their true ecliptic elements | The node from Horizons or the mission reports' state vectors | stand-in (node only) |
| `logs/departed/` (RS8, 462 logs from GCAT's phases) | Each phase is one piece: GCAT's periapsis, apoapsis and inclination around its body, with the orientation and the place along the orbit unknown (node and periapsis zero; a heliocentric orbit is turned to start where the craft left its last body). A heliocentric leg ending at a planet is a course solved from its ends, kept only when its perihelion and aphelion agree with GCAT's within 20%. Sun-Earth L1 and L2 are an orbit like the Earth's 1% inside or outside; Earth-Moon L1 and L2 a circle; a straight-in fall is a planned course. Moons and small bodies are not in the NAV fixture, so pieces around them have no position yet | GCAT is the only source covering every departed craft; it gives shapes, not state vectors | Horizons or SPICE state vectors per craft where they exist (Horizons holds about 200 of them) | accepted fudge. Measured 24 Nov 2026 (`departed_cruise_against_horizons`): solved courses (Mars Odyssey, MRO, MAVEN) 0.7 to 1.5% of distance from the Sun; turned orbits 0.4 to 5% (New Horizons, Rosetta), MESSENGER 19 to 23% (its many flybys change the orbit between GCAT's phases) |
| `logs/earth-orbit/` (RS8 part 2, stations and crewed flights) | One orbit per log from GCAT's satcat epoch, held fixed from launch to the end of the first phase; docking, reboosts and decay are left out, and a log whose first phase ends docked stops there ("docked to gcat:<id>"). Named landing sites without coordinates (KSC, Edwards, the Kazakh steppe) are placed from memory | satcat holds only the first phase, and GCAT's public tables keep no later ones Since RS8 part 3, a flight GCAT leaves docked lands on Wikidata's landing day (P620) at noon UTC, at its programme's usual zone (steppe, KSC, Dongfeng) where known; it stays docked until then, as undocking is in neither source  Since RS8 part 5, GCAT's end of mission (psatcat TDate) ends what docks: crewed ships land then (the hour too where GCAT gives it, which it does for most), cargo craft, modules and TKS burn up then; the undocking and deorbit burn are folded into that end, a fudge of hours for crewed ships and up to a few days for cargo craft. Recovery areas for sea splashdowns (Apollo north of Hawaii, Gemini in the western Atlantic, Dragon off Florida) and early Shenzhou's Siziwang are one point each, from memory; 4 landings with no site at all keep a stand-in point at 0, 0 | Mission timelines (press kits) for the undocking and each flight's own landing or splashdown point; TLE histories for the orbit's decay | stand-in |
| `apollo-11.json` orbit orientation | Parking and lunar orbits carry their size and inclination but node, periapsis and phase are zero | Written from the mission report's sizes before RS3 reads the state vectors | Node and phase from Horizons (the S-IVB, -399110, is there) or the report's state vectors | stand-in until RS3 |
| Release push direction from the place (CM2, `sse-nav/src/flightlog.rs` `push_direction`) | A release's separation push is taken along the flight for a front mount, against it behind, and straight out from the body orbited for the hull, a bay or a tether | The stack's attitude is not modelled anywhere yet, and players never hand-pilot; the push only seeds the released craft's first free orbit | The stack's attitude through the sequence (CM6) and the release mechanism's own axis on the mount | stand-in |
| Mount defaults in `packs/base-nav` `nav.mounts` (CM2) | A separation push of 0.5 m/s, a capture reach of 10 m and a capture speed of 0.3 m/s, used when the mount or the GM does not say | Compound ships design 4.1 and 6.3 gave them as editable defaults; no source read yet | Per mechanism: spring and pyrotechnic separation systems, docking-system contact limits (published per docking system) | estimated, unverified |

| Where | Instead of the physics | Why | Real physics to replace it | Status |
